Once you understand what you're building, present the design and get user approval.\n11: \n12: <HARD-GATE>\n13: Do NOT invoke any implementation skill, write any code, scaffold any project, or take any implementation action until you have presented a design and the user has approved it. This applies to EVERY project regardless of perceived simplicity.\n14: </HARD-GATE>\n15: \n16: ## Anti-Pattern: \"This Is Too Simple To Need A Design\"\n17: \n18: Every project goes through this process. A todo list, a single-function utility, a config change — all of them. \"Simple\" projects are where unexamined assumptions cause the most wasted work. The design can be short (a few sentences for truly simple projects), but you MUST present it and get approval.\n19: \n20: ## Checklist\n21: \n22: You MUST create a task for each of these items and complete them in order:\n23: \n24: 1. **Explore project context** — check files, docs, recent commits\n25: 2. **Offer visual companion** (if topic will involve visual questions) — this is its own message, not combined with a clarifying question. The ONLY skill you invoke after brainstorming is writing-plans.\n67: \n68: ## The Process\n69: \n70: **Understanding the idea:**\n71: \n72: - Check out the current project state first (files, docs, recent commits)\n73: - Before asking detailed questions, assess scope: if the request describes multiple independent subsystems (e.g., \"build a platform with chat, file storage, billing, and analytics\"), flag this immediately. Don't spend questions refining details of a project that needs to be decomposed first.\n74: - If the project is too large for a single spec, help the user decompose into sub-projects: what are the independent pieces, how do they relate, what order should they be built? Then brainstorm the first sub-project through the normal design flow. Each sub-project gets its own spec → plan → implementation cycle.\n75: - For appropriately-scoped projects, ask questions one at a time to refine the idea\n76: - Prefer multiple choice questions when possible, but open-ended is fine too\n77: - Only one question per message - if a topic needs more exploration, break it into multiple questions\n78: - Focus on understanding: purpose, constraints, success criteria\n79: \n80: **Exploring approaches:**\n81: \n82: - Propose 2-3 different approaches with trade-offs\n83: - Present options conversationally with your recommendation and reasoning\n84: - Lead with your recommended option and explain why\n85: \n86: **Presenting the design:**\n87: \n88: - Once you believe you understand what you're building, present the design\n89: - Scale each section to its complexity: a few sentences if straightforward, up to 200-300 words if nuanced\n90: - Ask after each section whether it looks right so far\n91: - Cover: architecture, components, data flow, error handling, testing\n92: - Be ready to go back and clarify if something doesn't make sense\n93: \n94: **Design for isolation and clarity:**\n95: \n96: - Break the system into smaller units that each have one clear purpose, communicate through well-defined interfaces, and can be understood and tested independently\n97: - For each unit, you should be able to answer: what does it do, how do you use it, and what does it depend on?\n98: - Can someone understand what a unit does without reading its internals? Can you change the internals without breaking consumers? If not, the boundaries need work.\n99: - Smaller, well-bounded units are also easier for you to work with - you reason better about code you can hold in context at once, and your edits are more reliable when files are focused. When a file grows large, that's often a signal that it's doing too much.\n100: \n101: **Working in existing codebases:**\n102: \n103: - Explore the current structure before proposing changes. Follow existing patterns.\n104: - Where existing code has problems that affect the work (e.g., a file that's grown too large, unclear boundaries, tangled responsibilities), include targeted improvements as part of the design - the way a good developer improves code they're working in.\n105: - Don't propose unrelated refactoring. Stay focused on what serves the current goal.\n106: \n107: ## After the Design\n108: \n109: **Documentation:**\n110: \n111: - Write the validated design (spec) to `docs/superpowers/specs/YYYY-MM-DD-<topic>-design.md`\n112:   - (User preferences for spec location override this default)\n113: - Use elements-of-style:writing-clearly-and-concisely skill if available\n114: - Commit the design document to git\n115: \n116: **Spec Self-Review:**\n117: After writing the spec document, look at it with fresh eyes:\n118: \n119: 1. **Placeholder scan:** Any \"TBD\", \"TODO\", incomplete sections, or vague requirements? Fix them.\n120: 2. **Internal consistency:** Do any sections contradict each other? Does the architecture match the feature descriptions?\n121: 3. **Scope check:** Is this focused enough for a single implementation plan, or does it need decomposition?\n122: 4. **Ambiguity check:** Could any requirement be interpreted two different ways? If so, pick one and make it explicit.\n123: \n124: Fix any issues inline. No need to re-review — just fix and move on.\n125: \n126: **User Review Gate:**\n127: After the spec review loop passes, ask the user to review the written spec before proceeding:\n128: \n129: > \"Spec written and committed to `<path>`. Please review it and let me know if you want to make any changes before we start writing out the implementation plan.\"\n130: \n131: Wait for the user's response. If they request changes, make them and re-run the spec review loop. Only proceed once the user approves.\n132: \n133: **Implementation:**\n134: \n135: - Invoke the writing-plans skill to create a detailed implementation plan\n136: - Do NOT invoke any other skill. writing-plans is the next step.\n137: \n138: ## Key Principles\n139: \n140: - **One question at a time** - Don't overwhelm with multiple questions\n141: - **Multiple choice preferred** - Easier to answer than open-ended when possible\n142: - **YAGNI ruthlessly** - Remove unnecessary features from all designs\n143: - **Explore alternatives** - Always propose 2-3 approaches before settling\n144: - **Incremental validation** - Present design, get approval before moving on\n145: - **Be flexible** - Go back and clarify when something doesn't make sense\n146: \n147: ## Visual Companion\n148: \n149: A browser-based companion for showing mockups, diagrams, and visual options during brainstorming. Available as a tool — not a mode. Accepting the companion means it's available for questions that benefit from visual treatment; it does NOT mean every question goes through the browser.\n150: \n151: **Offering the companion:** When you anticipate that upcoming questions will involve visual content (mockups, layouts, diagrams), offer it once for consent:\n152: > \"Some of what we're working on might be easier to explain if I can show it to you in a web browser. I can put together mockups, diagrams, comparisons, and other visuals as we go. This feature is still new and can be token-intensive. Want to try it? (Requires opening a local URL)\"\n153: \n154: **This offer MUST be its own message.** Do not combine it with clarifying questions, context summaries, or any other content. If they decline, proceed with text-only brainstorming.\n155: \n156: **Per-question decision:** Even after the user accepts, decide FOR EACH QUESTION whether to use the browser or the terminal. The test: **would the user understand this better by seeing it than reading it?**\n157: \n158: - **Use the browser** for content that IS visual — mockups, wireframes, layout comparisons, architecture diagrams, side-by-side visual designs\n159: - **Use the terminal** for content that is text — requirements questions, conceptual choices, tradeoff lists, A/B/C/D text options, scope decisions\n160: \n161: A question about a UI topic is not automatically a visual question. \"What does personality mean in this context?\" is a conceptual question — use the terminal. \"Which wizard layout works better?\" is a visual question — use the browser.\n162: \n163: If they agree to the companion, read the detailed guide before proceeding:\n164: `skills/brainstorming/visual-companion.md`\n165: \nThe above content shows the entire, complete file contents of the requested file.\n"}

Please note that any changes targeting the original code should remove the line number, colon, and leading space.\n1: ---\n2: name: llm-council\n3: description: Convene a council of independent agents that answer a hard question separately, blind-review and rank each other's answers, then synthesize one vetted answer. Fights sycophancy and one-angle reasoning — use when Jason wants a real answer instead of agreement. Two modes — default (5 Claude sub-agents) and cross-vendor (real GPT/Gemini/Claude/Grok via OpenRouter, faithful to karpathy/llm-council). Trigger on \"/llm-council\", \"convene the council\", \"run this through the council\", \"get a multi-model / second opinion\", \"stress-test this\", \"don't just agree with me\", \"argue both sides and tell me who's right\", \"is this actually a good idea\", or any moment he wants a decision pressure-tested rather than rubber-stamped. Use cross-vendor mode on \"cross-vendor\", \"real models\", \"different providers\", \"use openrouter\", \"faithful council\", or a `--cross-vendor` / `--openrouter` flag.\n4: ---\n5: \n6: # LLM Council\n7: \n8: A 3-stage pipeline (after karpathy/llm-council) that produces a vetted answer instead of a sycophantic one. Independent agents answer **blind**, then **blind-review and rank** each other's work, then the main loop (you, as Chairman) **synthesizes**. The win is blind peer review across independent reasoners — judge on merit, no one knows whose answer is whose.\n9: \n10: **The question** is whatever Jason passed (the `/llm-council` arg, or the thing he asked you to run through the council). If it's vague, ask one clarifying question first — a sharp question is worth more than five answers to a fuzzy one.\n11: \n12: ## Two modes — pick one before Stage 1\n13: \n14: - **Default (Claude council).** Five `opus` sub-agents, different lenses. Fast, free, no external calls, no question leaves Anthropic. This is what you run unless Jason asks for the other one. Follow **Stages 1–3** below.\n15: - **Cross-vendor (faithful council).** Real different providers — GPT, Gemini, Claude, Grok — via OpenRouter, exactly as karpathy/llm-council intended. Use it when Jason says \"cross-vendor\", \"real models\", \"different providers\", \"use openrouter\", \"faithful council\", or passes `--cross-vendor` / `--openrouter`. This **sends his question to OpenAI/Google/xAI** and costs money, so it's opt-in only — never silently upgrade the default into it. Jump to **Cross-vendor mode** near the bottom, then come back for Stage 3.\n16: \n17: The real win of cross-vendor is catching **correlated blind spots** — things every Claude gets wrong the same way. The default catches weak reasoning and one-angle answers but shares Claude's blind spots; the faithful council doesn't.\n18: \n19: ---\n20: \n21: ## Stage 1 — Independent answers (5 members, in parallel)\n22: \n23: Spawn **all five Agent calls in a single message** (one block, five tool calls) so they run concurrently. Each is a `general-purpose` agent on **`opus`** — every seat gets the smartest model. Give each the **same question** plus its lens. Members never see each other.\n24: \n25: | Member | model | Lens (what it's told to prioritize) |\n26: |---|---|---|\n27: | Pragmatist | `opus` | What actually works under real constraints. Bias to action and the concrete next move. |\n28: | Red-teamer | `opus` | Attack the premise. Where does this fail? Is the question itself wrong or missing something? |\n29: | Domain rigorist | `opus` | Technical correctness and precision. Name the real tradeoffs exactly; no hand-waving. |\n30: | First-principles | `opus` | Ignore convention and best-practice. Reason up from fundamentals; question defaults. |\n31: | Generalist | `opus` | Breadth. Connect angles, weigh the whole picture, answer plainly. |\n32: \n33: Diversity comes from the **lenses**, not from weaker models — a dumber model is noise, not a fresh perspective. Member count and lenses are easy to tune later.\n34: \n35: Prompt template for each member:\n36: \n37: > You are one member of an expert council answering a question independently. Your job is to give your **genuine best answer** — the lens below is what you should *emphasize*, not a character to perform.\n38: >\n39: > **Your lens:** {lens}\n40: >\n41: > **Question:** {question}\n42: >\n43: > Give a direct, well-reasoned answer. State your key assumptions and the strongest objection to your own position. Be specific and concise — no preamble, no hedging. You may use read-only tools if you genuinely need a fact to answer well, but lead with reasoning.\n44: >\n45: > Return only your answer.\n46: \n47: Collect the five answers verbatim.\n48: \n49: ---\n50: \n51: ## Stage 2 — Blind peer review + ranking (5 reviewers, in parallel)\n52: \n53: Label the five Stage-1 answers `Response A`, `Response B`, … `Response E` and **strip all identity** (no lens names, no model names). Keep your own private map of label → member for the notes later.\n54: \n55: Spawn **five reviewer Agent calls in one message**, all on **`opus`** (judging answer quality is harder than producing it — never cheap out on the judges). Each reviewer sees **all five anonymized answers**. (Reviewers are fresh stateless spawns — none can recognize \"its own\" answer, so there's no self-preference bias.)\n56: \n57: Reviewer prompt template:\n58: \n59: > You are evaluating anonymized answers to this question:\n60: >\n61: > **Question:** {question}\n62: >\n63: > {Response A … Response E, each as \"Response X:\\n{answer}\"}\n64: >\n65: > Your task:\n66: > 1. Evaluate each response individually — what it does well, what it does poorly — judging on **accuracy and insight only**, not style or length.\n67: > 2. Then give a final ranking, best to worst.\n68: >\n69: > Format the ranking EXACTLY like this at the very end:\n70: >\n71: > ```\n72: > FINAL RANKING:\n73: > 1. Response C\n74: > 2. Response A\n75: > 3. Response E\n76: > 4. Response B\n77: > 5. Response D\n78: > ```\n79: >\n80: > Only response labels in the ranking section — no extra text there.\n81: \n82: Parse each `FINAL RANKING:` block. Aggregate by average position (lower = better) to get the council's overall order.\n83: \n84: ---\n85: \n86: ## Stage 3 — Chairman synthesis (you, the main loop)\n87: \n88: You now hold all five answers and all five reviews. Do **not** just pick the #1 answer, and do **not** drift back toward whatever Jason originally implied — synthesize on **merit and consensus**.\n89: \n90: - Build the final answer from the strongest reasoning across all members, grafting good points even from low-ranked answers.\n91: - Where the council genuinely splits, **say so and take a position** — don't average the disagreement into mush.\n92: - If the Red-teamer (or anyone) showed the question's premise is wrong, **that leads.** The council exists to push back, not rubber-stamp.\n93: \n94: ---\n95: \n96: ## Output (respect the anti-fluff rules)\n97: \n98: Lead with the answer. Keep the council note tight.\n99: \n100: 1. **The answer** — the synthesized recommendation, stated plainly and directly.\n101: 2. **Council notes** — 3–5 lines max:\n102:    - where they agreed\n103:    - the one real disagreement (and which side you took, why)\n104:    - the aggregate ranking (e.g. `Pragmatist > Rigorist > First-principles > Generalist > Red-teamer`)\n105:    - anything you overrode and why\n106: \n107: Do **not** dump the five full answers by default. If Jason says \"show me each member\" / \"show the work,\" print the per-member answers and full reviews then.\n108: \n109: ---\n110: \n111: ## Cross-vendor mode (faithful council via OpenRouter)\n112: \n113: Only when Jason opted in (see \"Two modes\"). This replaces Stages 1–2 with a real multi-provider fan-out; **Stage 3 is unchanged** — you still synthesize as Chairman.\n114: \n115: The script `scripts/council_openrouter.py` (pure stdlib) does the work: it calls every council model in parallel for its independent answer (Stage 1), then sends each model all the **anonymized** answers and parses its `FINAL RANKING` (Stage 2), then prints one JSON blob — answers (de-anonymized back to their model), reviews, and the aggregate ranking.\n116: \n117: **Run it** from the project dir (so it finds `.env`):\n118: \n119: ```\n120: python3 ~/.claude/skills/llm-council/scripts/council_openrouter.py \"the exact question\"\n121: ```\n122: \n123: Default lineup is the original Karpathy council, editable as `COUNCIL_MODELS` at the top of the script:\n124: \n125: | Seat | OpenRouter slug |\n126: |---|---|\n127: | GPT | `openai/gpt-5.5` |\n128: | Gemini | `google/gemini-3.1-pro-preview` |\n129: | Claude | `anthropic/claude-opus-4.8` |\n130: | Grok | `x-ai/grok-4.3` |\n131: \n132: Then:\n133: \n134: 1. **Read the JSON.** `members[]` already maps each answer back to its real model. `aggregate_ranking[]` is best-first by average peer rank.\n135: 2. **Be Chairman (Stage 3, same rules as above).** Synthesize on merit and consensus across vendors — graft strong points even from low-ranked seats; where vendors genuinely split, say so and take a side; if any model showed the premise is wrong, that leads.\n136: 3. **Output (same format as default mode),** but in the council note name the **models** (`GPT > Claude > Grok > Gemini`), and call out anything where the vendors **diverged** — that divergence is the whole point of paying for this mode.\n137: \n138: Handling the script's output:\n139: - `error` field present → relay it. The common one is a missing `OPENROUTER_API_KEY`: tell Jason to add `OPENROUTER_API_KEY=sk-or-...` to the project `.env` (get it at openrouter.ai/keys), then re-run.\n140: - `failures[]` non-empty → one or more model slugs failed (often a renamed/retired slug returning HTTP 404). Note which seat dropped; if ≥2 members still answered, the council is still valid. Fix by editing `COUNCIL_MODELS`.\n141: - Want a single-model chairman instead of you? Pipe the question + answers to one more `--models` call (e.g. `--models \"google/gemini-3-pro-preview\"`). Not the default — you (the orchestrator with full context) are the better Chairman.\n142: \n143: ---\n144: \n145: ## Notes & limits\n146: \n147: - **Default mode shares Claude's blind spots.** Every member is Claude, so it catches weak reasoning, bad assumptions, and one-angle answers — but not blind spots all Claude models share. Reach for **cross-vendor mode** when the stakes justify the API cost and you specifically want a non-Claude check on the premise.\n148: - **Cross-vendor sends the question off-Anthropic** to OpenAI/Google/xAI via OpenRouter and costs real tokens. Opt-in only — never auto-upgrade the default.\n149: - Member count and lenses (default) / model list (cross-vendor) are easy to tune — the defaults are defaults, not laws. For a quick gut-check, 3 members is fine; for a high-stakes call, keep the full council.\n150: \nThe above content shows the entire, complete file contents of the requested file.\n"}

(`trailingSlash: 'always'`).\n\n---\n\n#### أقوى اعتراض على هذا الموقف:\nأن حذف الملفات القديمة قد يؤدي لكسر روابط خارجية (Backlinks) أو روابط داخلية مكتوبة بصيغة صلبة تشير مباشرة إلى ملفات الـ HTML
